in his hands and wrote the present work severely criticising 
Manorama. As the word Manorama is in the feminine in 
Sanskrit, he named his critique on it, Manoramakucamardana.

(4) Minava-nylya-Bastram, p. 43, No. 1230, "f.f, caJIed 
also Bra.hmokta-dharma-smrti in the first verse after the colo-
phon, is, the Satphita of Na.rada on the law-book of Manu. 
[Na.r8.da's work is often considered as an appendix to the work 
of Manu]. It is accompanied with a brief commentary- in the 
Newari language, entitled Nya.ya-vika.sini by Ma.l}ika, per-
haps the same as the author of Bhairavananda Na.ta,ka, who 
wrote thali work at the request of Jayata, the son of Candana 
Varma. and the minister of Jayasthiti Malla, the husband of 
Ra.jaJIa Devi, in N.S. 500, i.e., in 1380 A.D. 
The object was 
to bring the administration of justice more in unison with 
the Smrti th'an before. 
The advent of a new dynasty was 
followed by reforms in every direction, and the aim of these 
~eforms was Aryanizing the N ewars. 
The literary activity 
of this reign has been touched upon by Prof. Bendall: the 
administration of justice is spoken of here. The composition 
of many. new dramas, by Ma.l}ika, Dharma Gupta and others 
also bears witness to the tendency of Aryanizing. The pre-
sent MS. was copied by Vajra.ca.ryya Lilndabhadra, a 8ikya-
bhiqu belonging to the Klrttipu.l}ya Maha.viha.ra in BhatgAo. 
This shows that the Buddhist monks who excelled in Cali-
graphy were exten'sively employed in copying Hindu MSS.

which the present MS. contains the fourth, that dealing with

The medical tradition of ihis school seems to have begun

The King Yiithashpha is said to 
have been a worshipper of Gopalesvara, Sikharanarayal).a and

who protect the valley, namely, hananarayaJ;la Vil[lananarayana, 
Cal)gunarayal).a and SikharanarayaJ;la. From his title SiIpha 
he seems to have belonged to the race of Hari Silpha, and from

in Nepal. What stamps him as a King of Simraon is the 
epithet suraki-kula-kamala-kanana-vikasanaika-bhaskara, i.e., 
he helped in the establishment of the Surki dynasty of Jaunpore. 
The troubles in the then eastern (Shurq] dominion of Delhi 
commenced at the end of the reign of Sultan Firoz Shah of 
Delhi, 1888, and Yiltha SiIpha appears to be one of those Hindu 
Rajas who helped Malik Sharwar, a Khauja, to establish him-
self as Malik-us-shurq. Yiitha SiIpha seems to have been a 
contemporary and a relative of Jayasthiti who, by marrying 
Rajalla DevI, made himself master of Nepal. 
The present copy of the four-act RamayaJ;la is written in 
Newari some time after it was composed. Professor Bendall in 
his Cam. Cat. mentions a four-act RamayaJ;l8o entitled Ramanka 
natika by the same author, son of the same Ramadasa, com-
posed in 1860 A.D. 
This was enacted at Lalitapura. Rams.-
dasa was not a magistrate of Nepal as Professor Bendallsays, 
but he was the spiritual guide to the Kings of Ne.pal. 
The 
drama was composed when the poet was young and his father 
living. He composed it to please his father and, as is common 
with young aspirants of literary fame, he displays his sounding 
titles, VidylrJ}ava and BalavaglBvara. But the drama 

=== PAGE 43 ===

noticed in this Catalogue, p. 246, was enacted at a later period, 
in another part of Nepal. 
This composition is here represented 
as a Na~aka, but this is opposed to the rules of dramaturgy in 
which NMika only can be finished in four acts. Many years 
after its composition when the poet was an old man he gave 
up his sounding titles. 
This very drama appears to have 
been twice more enacted, once on the birth ceremony and once 
moro on the initation ceremony of Dharma Malla. 
The fact that the nandls and prastavanas differ accord-
ing to the occasion raises a nice question. 
The prastavanas 
are implicitly belif'ved to contain contemporary historical infor-
mation. That they certainly do. 
But theiplace and the occa-
sion mentioned in them relates only to one single representation 
during the author's lifetime. 
The information that this 
drama was enacted four times in 40 years or so, shows its 
immense popularity in Nepal at the end of the fourteenth 
century.

NityahQikatilakarp., pp. 45,47, and 111, Nos. 182011 and 
1If, is an unique work on the daily rites of the Tantrikas. The' 
MS. in p. 45 was copied in Newari character in N.S. 81'7= 
1197, the year of the Muhammadan conquest of Bihar; it was 
copied by .Icarya Harihara in the reign of Vijayakamadeva, 
alid that in 47 in N.S. 528=1403 A.D. The author is Mun-
jaka, also called J aya, the son of SrIkaQtha who was an adept 
in the Pascima Sasana. The ancient tantras are divided as be-
longing to north, south, east and west Sasanas. The worship 
of Kubjika and the Sastra Kubjikamata (for which see infra) be-
longs to the Pascima class. This work appears to be a very old 
compilation,-a handbook of the Kubjikamata. It says that 
the Saiva cult is higher than the Vedas, the DakllliQa is higher 
than the Saiva. The Pascima is the highest of all. The highest 
aim of this cult is said to be proficien~y in three kinds of Bhelaka 
or jugglery. The author's father, SrIkaQtha, appears to have 
been a great writer of this cult. It is difficult to say whether 
Jaya or Munjaka was a son or a disciple of SrIkal}tha. Per-
haps he was both, as the Nathas, though leading a monastic life, 
are credited with having children. The Nath~s or those who 
attained success in Tantrika cults were recruited from the low-
est classes and from outcastes, and their Sanskrit therefore is 
incorrect, ungrammatical and often unintelligible, as ia the 
case with the present work.

He has a sword in one hand and a MS. in the other; as the 
presiding god of all learning and skill he is also the god of 
arts, both fine and mechanical, and so the present work 
on Silpa is appropriately called MaiijusrisadhanaIp. It treats 
of such topics as temple architecture, construction of images, 
etc., making of domestic utensils, and so on. The writer of the 
work was absolutely ignorant of Sanskrit and writes in So 
language which is the language of practical SI}pagastra. under-
stood by the Silpa-practisers only.

As I have said elsewhere, the Mah~nirv~J.la tantra which 
attempts to regulate t~ntrika life as completely, as Manu does 
the Sm~rtta life, gives in the first chapter the description of 
a distinct religious literature partly vedic and partly t~ntrika. 
The writer says that this literature was held binding in the 
Dv~para Yuga. Then came Kali' in which the Agamas and 
the Nigamas obtained binding force. 
Scholars were very un-
willing to recognize the existence of this literature. But it 
appears to me that the Durbar Library contains at least two 
works which may safely be attributed to this literature, 
namely:-
Nisv~satattvasarphit~, pp. 10 and 187, No. 277, is written 
in transitional Gupta character which may be a century older, 
than the P~ramesvara tantra, copied in 859 A.D. 
The first 
half of the work is called Srauta and the second half Guhya. 
The leaves were in disorder. 
I have put them in order 
and it is a complete work running through 114 leaves and 
4,500 slokas. [See. Rep., p. 8.] 
The other sarphita work is variously termed Jay~khya 
sarphita, Jayak,arasarphit~ or J:fi~nalak~i. There are three 
MSS. of this work in the collection namely:-
Jay~k~arasarphit~, p. 1, No. 49; 
Jay~khyasarphit~ or 
Jn~nalak~mi, pp. 76 and 77, Nos. 1688 111 and.. The second 
in p. 76 is dated N.S. 807 = 1187 A.D. The others are not-
dated. The work is attributed to a human author S~dhaka 
Candra Datta who had received favour from 
Ek~yana. 
caryya Nar~yaQagarbha. The work is written in ungram-
matical Sanskrit throughout, and treats of mantrasandarpa!J,a. 
and other t~ntrika topics, as well as pretasrMdha antle§P, 
priyascitta, and other Bm~rtta topics.

W; 8rimatottara, p. 255, No. (2) 299. These with Nityllu;aika-

The works from want of 
time could not be prope.rly described. But the Government 
Collection of MSS·. in the Asiatic Society has recently been 
enriched by two copies of the Kubjikamata, one in Gupta and 
the other in Newari character. The former, I thought, was 
copied in the sixth century, but it may be a century later. An 
examination of these MSS. and the impressions left of the 
examination of the Durbar Library MSS., together with the 
meagre notes published in the Catalogue, have brought the 
following matters to light. 
There was a School of Tantra variously named Kubjiki-
mata, Kulalikamnaya, 8rImata, 
Kadimata 
Vidylpi~ha, 
Divyaugh~~sadbhava and so on. It had f3upplements, Uttaras 
named Srimatottara or Manthanabhairava and Kubjikl-
matottara, and it had its compilations, summaries, &c. The 
original is said to contain 24,000 slow divided into four parts, 
called ~a\kas· or six thousands each. Whether the Uttara is 
included in the 241,000 it is difficult to decide, b1}t the evidence 
available is in favour of their inclusion. In one place, how-
ever, the Ratna-pancaklvatira says that the Srimata-S8rlphitl 
is 12,000 only. The Government Gupta MS. says that the 
Vedas are not efficacious, and the work in 24,000 slokas is most 
effieacious, and of the 241,000 the first six thousand is the best 
The antiquity of the School is vouched by the facts that a

Jilanakarika, p. 79, No. 1684 QI', is a tantrika work of the 
Buddhists, attributed to Matsendranatha, commonly called 
Macchindranatha, whose temple at Patan is the greB.t resort 
of all Buddhists, in Nepal, and whose car festival is the great-
est festival in the valley. The work treats of such topics as 
Dharma and Adharma. 
Divyavadana, p. 89, No. 2 'I, contains many avadanas, 
notably Riipavati and not Riipavati as in Cowell's edition, 
Km.mlavadana., and 
Pa1psupradanavadana. 
'fhe reading 
often differs from the printed text. This is the only palm 
leaf MS. of the work known. 
Ni~panna-yogavali, p. 34, No. 1113 'I, contains 11 leaves 
in Newari character, copied in N.S. 686=1566 A.D., by 
Ravic:tndra, for his own use at the Sin-komagudi Vihara. 
'fhe scribe was a Vajracarya. The subject-matter treated of 
is the yoga practised by BhagavanBuddha with success. This 
appears to belong to the Vajrayana system of Buddhism as 
the Bhagavan Vajrasattva is represented as holding Vajra 
and sitting in the midst of a circle protected on all sides by 
Vajras. The great deity of this School is Vajrasattva, the high 
priest of the gods and represented as a form of Buddha. He 
is prominently mentioned in this work. 
The author is 
Abhayakaragupta. For another work of the same author see 
Bendall Cam.· Cat. p~ 197. He is called a Mahapal,l~ita. 
. 
Prayascitta Saucacaravidhi, p. 214, No. 1108, is a Bud. 
dhist work on expiation of sins· ·such as killing animals, kill-
ing Vajracaryas, killing BrahmRl,las, killing K~atriyas, &c., 
killing- cows, interc<;mrse with unclean tribes, eating unclean 
food, touching unclean things, purification and Sraddha. 
~l'his _ also appears, to be a york of the Vajrayana school of 
Buddhism. 'l'his is only a short compilation from. previ9us 
works on the subject. -The Buddhists seem to have borrowed 
:manyof the smrti practice$- of the Brahmal,l8S. 
Bodhicaryya:vatara, p~ 21, No. 772 'Ii. 'l'he MS. is a frag-
-;ment, only 7 leaves;--Its: _tippani, p.21; No! 772,11', contains 
notes on 
difficult passages only. These notes are often 
more useful than more ambitious commentaries.

The Catalogue to which the present Essay forms an introduction 
-is the result of a joint expedition to Nepal in the cold weather of 
1898.99 originally snggested by me, and taken part in by myself and 
Mahimahopidhyiya Haraprasid S'istri, accompanied under the aus-
pices of the Asiatic Society of Bengal, by his assistant, PaJ}cj.it Binoda-
vihiili Bhatticiryya. While co-operating with one another throughout, 
we arranged to divide generally our work 80 that the archmological1 
and historical part of the task should fall to myself, while the Pa.J}cJits 
dealt with the literary portion. 
.. 
A great deal of our time was of course taken up by the examination 
of the Maharaja's collection of MSS., which, as regards the antiquity 
of the documents, are surpassed by DO Sanskrit Library known to exist. 
Kyown necessarily very hurried examination of thia remarkable 001.

V. is a document of different orgin; it is a list of births of royal 
and other distinguished personages. 'I'he language is unfortunately 
old Newari; but one can make out the names and dates clearly enongh. 
These extend, not always in strict chronological order, from N.S. 177 
to 396. A specimen is given in the Plate, fig. 10. All the information 
given in this section of the Chronicle, so far as It relates to the kings, 
will be found condensed in the notes to the Table of kings below. 
Towards the end of the section other information besides births! deaths, 
and the like is introduced, but I have been able to make but little use 
of this owing to the difficulties of the language, for which I can get no 
adequate help either in Nepal or in Europe. 
Vs is perhaps merely a. continuation of VI. I have called it a.

Bhagavaulal's series which was issued by the son of Jayasthiti, Jyotir-

We may now turn back to aver y important point in this part of 
the history of Nepal, around which a good deal of misapprehension 
has gathered, namely the invasion of Harisi~ha. 
In spite of the boast of CaijQ.esvara, Harisirpha's minister that he was 
"victorious over all the kings of Nepal ",- thet'e seems to be at present 
no evidence beyond that of the VupsQvali·tradition preserved by Wright 
and Bhagavanliil to show that Harisirpha established himself in the 
valley of Nepal. Against this we may place the testimony of the new 
Vsl,!lSii.vali which was composed within about half a century of the 
event in question and (what is far more convincing) is confirmed by the 
colophoDs of several MSS. 
The precise nature of Harisirpha's expedi-
tion may be further explained by the Newan extract forming fig. ]0 of 
the Plllte; but mea.nwhile one can see that the effect of his expedition 
could hardly have been permanent, as not many years aftet' we find a. 
representative of the old royal family (Jayadeva)S on the throne. Until 
more evidence is forthcoming, it seems safer to regard Barisimha and 
his ancestors 40 who reigned in Tirhut,' Simraon and also possibly other 
parts of the Nepal-Tarai as at most titular kings of Nepal, even if 
they really claimed sovereignty over the valley of Nepal at aB.

eILrliest date (N.S. 500) is taken from the Cat. p. 43, where, beside the

1I.-WESTERN NEPAL. 
The town of Noakot or Nayakot (which I visited in my recent 
. tour) seems to have been a kind of frontier between the valley of 
Nepal or Nepal propel' and the Western districts. Wright's History (pp. 
223-5) mentions the seiznre of the place by a Gorkha sovereign pre-
vious to the general Gorkha Conquest. 
The MS. ill the Wright.collection nnmbered 1108 seems to haVE' 
been written here. For II1I1Iilw is doubtless (though the identification 
escaped me when I wrote my Cambridge Catalogue, p. 30) a Sanskri-
tized form of the town's name. The da.te of the king (Ratnajyotih) 
has been verified by Dr. Kielhorn and corresponds to January 14th, 
1392. 
During the following century no chronological data are forthcoming. 
I may note in passing that an educated Nepalese told me that inscribed 
stones, which he thought resembled those published by Pa\lcJit Bhagavan-
lil and myself, existed in the valleys of Western Nepal. 
Dr. Wright published (History, Chapter XII) an account of the 
reigning (Gorkha) dynasty from Dravya Siih (A. D. 1559) to tbe pre-
sent time. 
It is interesting to find in the present collection (pp. 242-4) a 
MS. giving confirmation of this record. It was composed by Rajen-
dravikrama Sib, who reigned 1816-1847. It might be worthwhile 
to publish extracts from this MS., when furthel' confirmatory material 
(fa'om old MSS. or inscriptions) comes to light. Meanwhile, it is worth 
noting that Wright's date, 1559, founded on a Vikrama-date, is curiously 
corroborated by a chl'onogram (vidku-tJasu-nigama-glau I), which gives 
the ('orresponding S'aka year (1481). At p. 213 of the Catalogue we 
find a.. MS. written during the reign of Varavira. Sibi, in 1614, at 
Jatipatta.na. This looks like S'aka1614 (A.D. 1692), when Virabhadra 
Sihi was alive, thougb, according to Wright, he was only yUtJaraj a.nd 
never mahll'l'iJja. B

statement. For it seems unlikely that Anantamalla should not only have superseded an older heir· apparent, but also have allowed him
